Abstract: The $5d^5$ Ruddlesden-Popper iridates Sr$_2$IrO$_4$ and Ba$_2$IrO$_4$ have attracted considerable attention as spin-orbit entangled analogues of the cuprates, yet the origin of their antiferromagnetic insulating state, of Slater or of Mott-Heisenberg type, remains debated. We find that both materials sit in an intermediate regime in which itinerant and localized electrons cooperate in stabilizing the antiferromagnetic order, with Ba$_2$IrO$_4$ slightly more correlated than Sr$_2$IrO$_4$. We obtain this by computing, within dynamical mean-field theory, the interaction-temperature phase diagram of a realistic three-orbital $t_{2g}$ Hubbard model with spin-orbit coupling. Our study shows that neither the behavior of the gap across the Néel temperature nor the spin-polaron features of the spectral function unambiguously identify the cooperative regime, whereas the two-peak structure of the optical conductivity is reproduced only there and therefore constitutes a reliable fingerprint. By placing both compounds in a common phase diagram, our results reconcile seemingly contradictory experimental and theoretical reports, and provide a unified framework for future studies of the 214 iridates and of other spin-orbit coupled correlated materials.

</details>

Abstract: We establish an exact mechanism of magnetic confinement driven purely by kinetic frustration. In a finite open sawtooth $U=\infty$ Hubbard chain of $L$ unit cells doped with one hole, the ground state at magnetization $M$ contains $M$ polarized triplet dimers surrounding a connected singlet region of length $\ell_{\rm s}=L-M$. A magnetic field selects $M$ and hence the confinement length. The hole forms a standing wave in the singlet region with exponential tails in the triplet background. Confinement requires neither disorder nor an exchange-generated string tension: destructive interference between direct and apex-mediated hopping makes the triplet bonds reflecting. At $t_2/t_1=[2+4\cos(π/(\ell_{\rm s}+2))]^{-1/2}$, they become perfectly reflecting, producing a position-degenerate compact localized state. At every fixed field $0<h<h_{\rm sat}$, the singlet region remains finite as $L\to\infty$ and shrinks stepwise with increasing field, whereas at $h=0$ it spans the system.

</details>

Abstract: The spin-1/2 kagome Heisenberg antiferromagnet (AFM) provides a paradigmatic platform for realizing quantum spin-liquid (QSL) and magnetization-plateau states. Experimental progress, however, has been hindered by structural disorder, whose effects on these quantum states remain elusive. A key experimental challenge is therefore to connect intrinsic and disorder-dominated regimes through controlled disorder and identify emergent degrees of freedom associated with disorder. Here we introduce controlled point defects through electron irradiation into the spin-1/2 kagome AFM YCu3(OH)6.5Br2.5 (YCOB) and track the resulting evolution of its low-energy magnetic states. High-field magnetic torque measurements reveal that the 1/9 and 1/3 magnetization plateaus are strongly suppressed by only ~0.05% irradiation-induced defects, demonstrating that these delicate quantum many-body states are extraordinarily fragile against dilute disorder. Moreover, the irradiation induces a sign reversal of the magnetic torque only at low temperatures and low fields, indicating the emergence of correlated defect-induced moments rather than isolated free spins. Remarkably, the characteristic scaling of the magnetic specific heat, absent in pristine YCOB, progressively emerges with increasing disorder. These findings establish dilute disorder as an effective tuning parameter that drives the kagome AFM from intrinsic quantum states toward a disorder-dominated regime. The contrasting responses further identify pristine YCOB as an exceptionally clean kagome AFM for accessing intrinsic quantum states.

</details>

Abstract: Controlling charge--spin conversion within a single magnetic material is important for compact spintronic devices. Using first-principles calculations, symmetry analysis, and Wannier-based linear response, we study the Edelstein and spin Hall responses of sliding-ferroelectric bilayer \ce{Fe3GaTe2}. Two degenerate ground states with opposite interlayer sliding vectors and out-of-plane dipole moments are separated by a calculated switching barrier of about $16.3$~meV per unit cell. Magnetization orientation determines the allowed current-induced spin components, while ferroelectric switching induces component-selective sign reversals governed by $M_z\mathcal T$ for in-plane magnetization and by $M_z$ for out-of-plane magnetization. The Edelstein response is strongly energy dependent, with spin accumulation approaching $2~\hbar$ for $\mathbf m\parallel+y$ under a reference field of $1$~V/Å. Momentum-resolved analysis links the TR-even and TR-odd Edelstein responses to $Γ$-centered constant-energy contours and localized interband features, respectively. Spin-conductivity contributions show the opposite momentum-space character, with broad sign-competing spin Berry curvature for TR-even and narrow Fermi-surface contours for TR-odd. The transverse spin Hall conductivity reaches $\sim10^{3}\,(\hbar/e)(\mathrm{S/cm})$ and depends strongly on ferroelectric state and magnetization direction. Magnetization rotations in the $xy$ and $xz$ planes reveal symmetry-allowed higher angular harmonics in both spin accumulation and spin-current responses. These results establish sliding-ferroelectric bilayer \ce{Fe3GaTe2} as a tunable platform for reconfigurable spin accumulation and spin-current generation.

</details>

Abstract: Flat-band systems provide a promising platform for exploring exotic correlated phases owing to quenched kinetic energy and enhanced interaction effects. In this work, we investigate the flat-band superfluid transition in the attractive kagome Hubbard model using large-scale determinant quantum Monte Carlo simulations accelerated by fast Fourier transforms and delay updates. Through systematic calculations of the transition temperature, spin susceptibility, and density compressibility, we establish the finite-temperature phase diagram at the flat-band filling $ρ=5/3$ and find a clear linear scaling of the superfluid transition temperature with the interaction strength, $T_{\mathrm{c}}=0.0314|U|$. Our results reveal a wide separation of temperature scales between local pair formation and global phase coherence, which stabilizes a broad intermediate supercompressible bosonic fluid region of preformed pairs that bridges the normal Fermi gas and the flat-band superfluid. We further demonstrate that both the transition temperature and density compressibility are significantly enhanced in the flat band compared to van Hove fillings. Our findings highlight the essential role of flat-band-enhanced pair-density fluctuations in separating the pairing and phase-coherence scales, offering clear experimental signatures for probing flat-band superfluidity and superconductivity in cold-atom and kagome-material platforms.

</details>

Abstract: We investigate the $1/9$ magnetization plateau of the spin-$1/2$ kagome Heisenberg antiferromagnet using infinite projected entangled-pair states and identify a spin-density-wave state with a $27$-site unit cell and a lower variational energy than previous approaches. In contrast to higher-magnetization plateaus, which are well described by localized magnons on nonoverlapping hexagons and preserve a sixfold rotational symmetry, the $1/9$ plateau only keeps a threefold rotational symmmetry and consists of a tiling by three types of $9$-site clusters related by $\pm2π/3$ rotations. These conclusions are supported by a complementary cluster mean-field theory which correctly preserves the symmetry of the higher plateaus but leads to the same spontaneous symmetry breaking for the $1/9$ plateau with a qualitatively similar pattern of nine different local magnetizations.

Abstract: In order to identify intrinsic quantum resources in strongly interacting fermionic systems, we introduce a symmetry resolution of the parity-superselected two-site entanglement, as measured by both the fermionic negativity and the relative entropy of entanglement. We discuss how the symmetry resolution can be extended also to the full entanglement in pure and mixed two-site states. We apply our recipe to the Hubbard model, first for a dimer, and then for a plaquette cluster dynamical mean-field theory, an established method tailored to accurately capture short-range correlations. Following the evolution of the model through the interaction- and density-driven Mott transitions at zero temperature, we highlight how the three symmetry-resolved components of the two-site entanglement probe different key aspects of Mott and pseudogap physics.

Abstract: We investigate spinon--spinon interactions in the quasi-one-dimensional $S=1/2$ Heisenberg antiferromagnet Cu(C$_4$H$_4$N$_2$)(NO$_3$)$_2$ (CuPzN) using inelastic neutron scattering. At zero field, the spectrum exhibits the canonical multi-spinon continuum of the Heisenberg chain. With increasing field we demonstrate the appearance of an energy gap $2Δ$ towards momentum $q\rightarrow 0$ between the uniform magnetization precession mode and the shifted spinon continuum. $2Δ$ is a direct measure of spinon backscattering interactions. Comparison with numerical matrix-product-state calculations and a recent analytical theory shows excellent agreement. By examining the integrable spin-1/2 chain, we provide an example of how quasiparticle interactions can be quantified experimentally in isotropic quantum spin systems.

Abstract: When electrons move through non-trivial spin textures in magnetic metals, conduction electrons couple to magnetic order through both their charge and their spin, acquiring non-trivial equations of motion and a dynamic spin polarization providing the foundation of spintronics. These mechanisms are, however, almost invariably reciprocal and the resistance a current encounters does not depend on its flow direction. Here we show that a chiral crystal lattice and an independently reversible chiral magnetic order together produce a diode whose forward direction is set by the magnetism rather than by the structure. In the triangular-lattice antiferromagnet Co$_{1/3}$TaS$_2$, whose chiral lattice hosts a chiral magnetic order of its own, independent handedness, we observe a pronounced nonreciprocal resistance. Unlike conventional electrical magnetochiral anisotropy, which is driven by an external magnetic field, our spontaneous magnetic diode effect arises from an internal noncoplanar spin texture with vanishing net magnetization. Symmetry analysis and model calculations show that the effect originates from the interplay of noncoplanar spin order and spin-orbit coupling, which generates a chiral, time-reversal-symmetry-breaking electronic response. Crucially, reversing the magnetic handedness reverses the sign of the nonreciprocal resistance, demonstrating magnetic control of the diode polarity.

</details>

Abstract: Finite-size effects are ubiquitous in quantum matter, yet typically invisible in the thermodynamic limit; when system size is restricted, boundaries and confinement reveal modified spectra, correlations, and emergent phenomena. With growing access to well-controlled few-spin systems, these effects are now directly observable and offer a route to probing universal physics and enabling quantum information applications. Here, we study finite-size physics in the quasi-one-dimensional Ising-like quantum antiferromagnet Ba(Co$_{1-x}$Mg$_x$)$_2$V$_2$O$_8$ with $x = 2\%$ and $x = 5\%$, where non-magnetic Mg$^{2+}$ ions fragment the Co$^{2+}$ spin chains into finite segments. Combining inelastic neutron scattering and numerical calculations, we identify distinct regimes with fundamentally different low-energy quantum excitations: spinons confined to chain boundaries, and a cluster mode in shorter chains corresponding to a collective reversal of the Néel-ordered segment without classical analogue. Our results demonstrate how chemical substitution acts as a quantum microscope providing direct access to boundary-shaped excitations in strongly correlated spin systems.

</details>

Abstract: Solid oxygen provides a unique platform for studying the interplay between structural and magnetic degrees of freedom, as each O$_2$ molecule possesses an $S=1$ spin and weak intermolecular van der Waals interactions allow substantial structural flexibility. In addition, because of its small intermolecular hopping integrals, this system is known to be a strongly correlated electron system with a multiorbital nature. In this study, we examine electronic states and magnetic and dielectric responses using a multiorbital Hubbard model with Peierls-type electron-lattice coupling. This model provides a minimal description of the interplay between structural and magnetic degrees of freedom in solid oxygen. We apply the Hartree-Fock approximation to obtain the ground-state phase diagram. Antiferromagnetic order appears when the onsite Coulomb interaction is strong, whereas three nonmagnetic bond-ordered states emerge when it is weak. At intermediate interaction strengths, antiferromagnetic order coexists with each of the three bond orders. These three coexistence phases all break time-reversal symmetry; one preserves spatial inversion symmetry, whereas the other two also break it. The two inversion-breaking phases exhibit linear magnetoelectric responses, whereas the inversion-symmetric phase exhibits altermagnetic spin splitting and a piezomagnetic response. These responses may provide experimental signatures of coexisting magnetic and structural orders in high-pressure solid oxygen suggested by recent experiments.

</details>

Abstract: Geometrical flat bands provide an ideal platform for studying electronic correlations and quantum-geometric effects. Although the ferromagnetic Weyl semimetal \CoSnS is generally considered a moderately correlated system, recent spectroscopy experiments have revealed a flat-band feature near the Fermi level ($\EF$) that is absent in conventional density functional theory (DFT) calculations. Using DFT plus dynamical mean-field theory and a minimal kagome model, we show that this feature arises not from heavy-fermion-like dynamical band renormalization, but from the shifting of a $d_{x^2-y^2}$ dominated geometrical kagome flat band toward $\EF$ driven by orbital- and spin-dependent self-energies. The relocated flat band enhances the local Berry curvature near $\EF$, resulting in a strongly energy-dependent anomalous Hall conductivity and a giant anomalous Nernst effect. Our findings establish an orbital-selective correlation mechanism for positioning geometrical flat bands at $\EF$, and propose anomalous thermoelectric transport as a sensitive probe of flat band induced topology.

</details>

Abstract: The relevance of spin, lattice and orbital degrees of freedom in the quasi-2D Ising-like magnet Li$_2$FeSiO$_4$ is demonstrated by thermal expansion and specific heat measurements supported by numerical studies. By means of thermodynamic and Grüneisen analysis, we separate the orbital signatures and quantify the energy gap and its pressure dependence from the experimental data as $Δ\sim 300 - 350$ K and $\partial \ln Δ/\partial p_{\rm c} \approx -4.6$ %/GPa). Both values excellently agree with the theoretical DFT predictions. The thermal expansion anomalies associated with the onset of long-range magnetic order imply significant magneto-elastic coupling and are used to determine the anisotropic uniaxial strain dependencies of $T_{\rm N}$. They are dominated by the in-plane (i.e., $c$ axis) response $(\partial \ln{ T_{\rm N}} )/\partial p_{\rm c} \simeq 15$ %/GPa while the (negative) $a$ axis and (positive) out-of-plane ($b$ axis) pressure dependencies are much smaller. Abstract: We generalize the previous group-theory based classification of collinear magnetism in the quasicrystal (QC) to general magnetic states. Taking the Penrose lattice as an example, we find that all magnetic states belonging to 2D irreducible representations (IRRPs) of the $D_5$ point group, including collinear states and coplanar ones, correspond to altermagnetism (AM). Unlike conventional collinear AM wherein zero magnetism is protected by, e.g. rotation or mirror reflection, which globally flip the spin in the magnetic pattern, the zero magnetism in these 2D-IRRP states is instead protected by a special group-theory-orthogonality theorem that basis functions belonging to different IRRPs are always mutually orthogonal. Taking the single-orbital Penrose-Hubbard as an example, our linear-response theory based calculations show that the largest portion of the parameter space is occupied by these 2D-IRRP states, and our further Ginzburg-Landau theory based analysis reveals that the polarization directions of the two basis magnetic states in the 2D IRRPs can be either parallel or perpendicular, leading to collinear or coplanar magnetic states. The special group-theory-orthogonality compensation mechanism and the absence of the PT symmetry finally leads to AM states, which are to be verified by the spin split in the spin-dependent angle-resolved-photoemission-spectrum and the spin conductance. These intriguing collinear and coplanar AM can also emerge in other QCs.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, we determine the complete ground-state spaces for a broad class of generalized stabilizer quantum models defined over CW-decompositions of non-compact manifolds of arbitrary dimensions and topological type. We introduce a $C^*$-algebraic framework in which the quantum spin algebra $\A$ is realized as the twisted group $C^*$-algebra of an infinite discrete Pauli module $P$ endowed with a symplectic structure. We establish a correspondence between stabilizer models and isotropic subgroups of $P$: Every Lagrangian extension of such a subgroup determines a $C^*$-diagonal of $\A$, whose Weyl groupoid encodes the dynamics through a continuous 1-cocycle. This realization yields an explicit family of extremal ground-states for every admissible Lagrangian model, together with their decomposition into sectors. When the sector space is $T_0$, this gives a complete characterization of the ground-state spaces. We also prove that every Lagrangian stabilizer model admits a unique frustration-free ground state with a spectral gap. Our framework applies in arbitrary dimension, including higher abelian quantum doubles, homological codes, and a broad class of stabilizer models.

</details>

Abstract: We define and study the notion of partial Airy ideals, which are certain linear systems of PDEs that may contain less equations than variables. We show that they admit a unique solution of exponential type (the partition function) modulo a choice of initial condition. We construct a large class of examples of partial Airy ideals attached to spectral curves using twisted free-field representations of $\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{gl}_r)$-algebras at self-dual level. This generalizes the results of Bouchard- Borot-Chidambaram-Creutzig-Noshchenko by significantly enlarging the set of admissible spectral curves, provided they come with additional data that we call colour. We show that the differential constraints on the partition function are equivalent to a set of equations on the associated correlators, which we call coloured loop equations. We find a recursive residue formulation for the solutions of those equations, the coloured blobbed topological recursion}, which is a generalization of the currently known forms of topological recursion. It includes the blobbed topological recursion of Borot-Shadrin as a special case, giving it a natural interpretation within the formalism of partial Airy ideals.

</details>

Abstract: Blood vessels remodel in response to wall shear stress while the network operates
  under a metabolic maintenance budget. For a power-law maintenance cost, minimizing
  dissipation at fixed budget yields a local shear set-point, and a purely local adaptation rule
  drives the network towards this optimum. Here we give this result a non-equilibrium thermodynamic
  formulation. Blood flow is an exactly linear Onsager system, and the slow remodeling of vessel radii
  is a gradient flow generated by a Rayleigh dissipation functional. This identifies the structural
  mobility, which scales inversely with the maintenance cost of each vessel and can be measured from
  radius fluctuations. Enforcing the budget exactly requires a time-dependent metabolic multiplier,
  which we obtain in closed form; with a fixed multiplier the budget is violated by up to one third
  during transients. The fluid displaced by remodeling vessels couples flow and structure through a
  symmetric, non-diagonal mobility, so that Onsager reciprocity becomes a testable statement:
  neighbouring vessels should show anticorrelated radius fluctuations. With noise, the structural
  sector obeys a second law and an integral fluctuation theorem. Non-reciprocal signaling between
  vessels, such as upstream conducted responses, then breaks the time-reversal
  symmetry of radius fluctuations and produces housekeeping entropy, detectable from time series of
  realistic length. Finally, we construct a partition function over network
  topologies with the number of loops as order parameter. Numerical tests show that loop counts do
  not collapse onto a single curve against an effective temperature, and that they
  depend strongly on the detection threshold.

</details>

Abstract: We consider a classical Coulomb gas of $N$ particles in dimension $d\geq2$, confined by a general external potential $U$ in the large-$N$ limit. In that limit the density of particles has a compact support called the droplet. We study smooth linear statistics ${\mathcal L}_N=\sum_{i=1}^N f({\bf x}_i)$ at large $N$. While the leading variance of ${\mathcal L}_N$ is by now well understood in many settings, much less is known about higher cumulants. When $f({\bf x})=f(|{\bf x}|)$ and the potential is rotationally invariant, these cumulants were computed recently at leading order. We derive explicit formulas for the leading third and fourth cumulants for general smooth droplets, without assuming that either the potential or the test function is radial. The answers are expressed as intrinsic boundary integrals over the equilibrium droplet. The third cumulant depends on the cube of the jump of the normal derivative of the harmonic extension of $f$, while the fourth cumulant also involves the exterior Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator, the mean curvature of the boundary, and normal derivatives of the background density. Our derivation uses the tilted potential $U_s=U+s f/β$, the associated deformation of the droplet, and shape derivatives of the Dirichlet energy. This deformation has a natural interpretation as the infinitesimal motion of the free boundary in the obstacle-problem formulation of the equilibrium measure. Our formulas extend previous studies by Wiegmann and Zabrodin in two dimensions for $β=2$. In their setting, we provide a rigorous proof of the limiting third and fourth cumulants, supporting the proposed formulas for general Coulomb gases. We specialize the general formulas to the disk, the half-plane and the sphere, and check them against exactly solvable non-radial deformations, including translated elliptic droplets in $d=2$ and spheroidal deformations in $d=3$.

Abstract: The triangle map is an area-preserving, piecewise-parabolic map of the torus with vanishing Lyapunov exponent. We study the symbolic process $X_n=\pm1$ given by the two halves of the torus, whose partial sums $S_n$ are the transport of the lifted momentum, with an integrator free of accumulated rounding errors and exact computations on polygonal cylinder sets. We prove that the Koopman operator has no non-constant $H^1$ eigenfunctions, that the symbol is not an $H^1$ coboundary, and that runs of equal symbols are uniformly bounded. For generic parameters the walk is normally diffusive and Gaussian, but its diffusion coefficient is not that of a coin: away from the edges of the parameter square it varies between $0.2$ and $14$ times the Bernoulli value, and for a benchmark pair, with $D=3.09\pm0.01$, the excess is produced by the order of the bounded inter-event intervals, not by their distribution. The first-passage exponent is $1/2$ with a non-Bernoulli amplitude, and worst-case Birkhoff averages decay as for a uniquely ergodic system. For $β=0$ the Green-Kubo sum cancels, $D=0$, yet $\mathrm{Var}(S_n)$ keeps growing slowly, sign changes are rare and the correlations of Fourier modes decay as $n^{-0.2}$. We identify the zero-sum words, which become pure twists at $β=0$, as resonances that trap orbits with a computable $1/\ell$ law, set the crossover time $β^{-1/2}$ at small $β$, and, in an asymmetric deformation of the symbol, produce a logarithmic term in the variance whose coefficient we compute exactly. We formulate conjectures on unique ergodicity, mixing, the central limit theorem and the cohomology of the symbol.

Abstract: Long-range correlations in written texts extend over scales far beyond local syntax, but their relation to semantic organisation remains incompletely understood. Previous work has linked these correlations to the bursty recurrence of topically relevant words. Here we generalise that construction from individual lexical items to distributed semantic observables. We map a text token by token onto a trajectory of pre-trained word embeddings, use principal component analysis (PCA) to identify text-specific directions of semantic variation, and threshold the projected series to define inter-event times; their coefficients of variation form a component-resolved burstiness spectrum $\{B_k\}$. Across 28 literary and scientific texts and the first 20 components, the originals remain systematically above the geometric inter-event-time baseline and above two complementary controls: a word-shuffled null, which preserves the embedding covariance, the PCA eigensystem and the marginal projected distributions while destroying temporal order, and a fractional-Gaussian-noise surrogate, which preserves the Zipfian frequency structure and matches the detrended fluctuation analysis (DFA) exponent of the original rank sequence. With events defined by the upper 5% tail of each projection, the original exceeds both controls in every text-component pair, while the two controls are statistically indistinguishable from each other. Autocorrelation and detrended fluctuation analysis of the semantic projections separate the originals from both controls in the same way. Global rank-level long-range correlations are therefore insufficient to generate the bursty recurrence observed along semantic directions. The results instead support an interpretation in terms of the ordered recurrence of semantically related material.

</details>

Abstract: Classical shell fracture theories predominantly consider cracks aligned with principal curvature directions in cylindrical and spherical shells under tensile mode or pressure loading, leaving the fracture behavior of arbitrarily oriented cracks in doubly curved shells under general loading poorly understood. We show that shell curvature couples opening, sliding, and tearing, allowing a single loading component to activate multiple fracture modes, unlike in flat plates. This coupling depends strongly on crack orientation and the normal, parallel, and torsional components of the shell curvature. Ellipsoids exhibit weak coupling, hyperboloids generally exhibit the strongest coupling, and cylinders display the most complex orientation-dependent responses. Moreover, in-plane shear can induce crack closure in hyperboloidal shells, suggesting a potential geometric mechanism for enhanced damage resistance. These findings elucidate the role of local curvature in fracture-mode coupling and provide a mechanistic foundation for the analysis of shellular (meta-)structures with engineered curvature distributions.

Abstract: We propose a simple realization of an open quantum battery powered by local dephasing. The setup consists of a triple-quantum-dot chain coupled at one end to a thermal reservoir, with the dot at the opposite end strongly detuned to form a high-energy storage element. Local dephasing populates this highly excited state and autonomously drives the system into a nonequilibrium steady state with high ergotropic efficiency. We find a limit where the ergotropic efficiency approaches unity, while the ergotropy itself approaches one-third of the energy detuning. We characterize the battery through its energy-storage performance, charging time, and stability, showing that the charged state remains long-lived after the dephasing source is switched off. We then demonstrate two complementary protocols for extracting the stored energy. First, nearly all the available ergotropy can be recovered through a simple cyclic unitary protocol involving only modulation of the detuned-dot gate. Second, coupling the detuned dot to an electronic load allows the extraction of electrochemical work exceeding the initial ergotropy, thereby accessing part of the battery's passive energy. Our results establish local dephasing as an autonomous resource for charging open quantum batteries and demonstrate simple routes for extracting the resulting stored energy in experimentally accessible mesoscopic platforms.

Abstract: Geometric tailoring of magnetic responses is a central theme in modern magnetism. By employing cross-sectional geometric modulation, we investigate how geometric variations influence the dynamics of field-driven domain walls. Theoretical calculations based on a biaxial ferromagnet model and a collective-variable approach are in excellent agreement with micromagnetic simulations. Abstract: Phase transitions can generate intricate spatial organization invisible to spatially averaged probes, yet quantitative measures of this organization remain limited. Here we develop "entropy imaging," an information-theoretic technique that harnesses lossless compression to quantify spatial complexity, predictability, and order in quantum materials. Using this approach, emergent spatial textures can be imaged and converted into an experimentally-accessible observable: the computable information density (CID), a compression-based measure of information complexity that approaches the Shannon entropy rate in the large-system limit. Applied to millikelvin microwave impedance microscopy of graphene, the method reveals a pronounced maximum in the CID near quantum Hall transitions, a quantitative measure of the information-rich spatial organization of the critical landscape. A disordered Hofstadter model reproduces this behavior and shows that the CID can identify the quantum critical point from a sequence of images, without ensemble averaging over disorder realizations. We further show that the geometric information encoded in CID is not reducible to a correlation length, and it can even distinguish spatial textures with identical multifractal statistics. Together, these results establish lossless compression as a framework for quantifying emergent spatial organization in quantum materials, with portability across imaging modalities and physical systems.

The static structure factor and the effective hop rate are obtained in closed form, exactly along a one-dimensional channel; with the sum rule they give a single exponential, a lower bound on the ISF. Exact relaxation spectra show that this single exponential, built from equilibrium quantities alone, reproduces the correlation time only at weak coupling; only there can a measured dephasing rate be read through the statics. For strong attraction the slow dissolution of clusters lengthens the correlation time up to ninefold, whereas a repulsive layer relaxes nearly as a single mode at its structure peak. The dephasing rate, fitted over a time window where it is nearly constant, approaches the slowest relaxation rate, not the initial one; the sum rule reaches the data only through the area beneath the normalized decay, which defines the correlation time. For CO/Cu(001), the lattice gas reproduces the dephasing rates measured along two crystal directions and requires a nearest-neighbour repulsion of at least $35$--$50$~meV, whatever the jump rates; the measured rate is then close to the inverse correlation time, and the memory is predicted to grow with coverage.

We derive exact optimal resource-allocation protocols for informed search processes in which the agent has continuous access to its past and current positions, which we find to always require resource allocation to be spatially concentrated. We then determine the corresponding protocols for blind searchers and show that resource usage must be more evenly distributed. Non-Markovian cues that generate environmental memory are found to substantially improve search performance when the agent has access to positional information, even under finite-resource constraints. In contrast, their benefits are considerably more limited for blind search, particularly when the cues provide no directional information.

Abstract: We establish a reference modeling framework for random probability distributions subject to constraints. Generalizing the hyperensemble approach of Crooks, we formulate Jaynesian priors of maximal uncertainty over the unit simplex of categorical distributions. This framework is entirely general, but we primarily investigate models with constraints on physically relevant quantities, namely, average energy and Shannon entropy. A constraint on the exact value of average energy uniquely determines a Boltzmann-Gibbs equilibrium, but also defines a hyperplanar subset of the probability simplex consisting of all distributions with that value of average energy. The associated hyperensemble has a uniform probability density across all such distributions, regardless of distance to equilibrium. Such reference models make predictions of generic nonequilibrium properties such as the mean and modal microstate distributions and the expected free energy of a random distribution, which we examine analytically and via exact sampling algorithms. Moreover, introducing constraints on Shannon entropy permits parametric access to tunably nonequilibrium regimes. The approach follows conventional Jaynesian modeling logic and is compatible with standard Bayesian inference, and reduces to Boltzmann-Gibbs statistics when Shannon entropy is maximized. We examine the physical predictions of this scheme, and demonstrate its wide compatibility with other approaches to nonequilibrium modeling. We illustrate the framework in three scenarios: (i) comparison to superstatistics quasi-equilibrium modeling, (ii) generation of random nonequilibrium steady states, and (iii) Bayesian inference under a prior hyperensemble. We highlight numerous prospects for theoretical extensions and empirical application.

Abstract: We study the extremal and global-time statistics of a one-dimensional sluggish random walk, whose diffusivity decays as a power law of the distance from the origin, under Poissonian resetting to the origin. A change of variable maps the process onto a signed Bessel process, so that renewal theory applies. We obtain the exact Laplace transform of the distribution of the maximum over a finite window. At long times the centered maximum of the transformed process follows the Gumbel law, and in physical space the typical maximum grows as a power of the logarithm of the window length. The far-tail rate function does not depend on $α$ in the Bessel variable, so the second-order dynamical transition known for Brownian resetting, between maxima produced by a single anomalously long inter-reset interval and maxima produced by an interval occupying essentially the whole window, holds for every $α$. The time of the maximum is uniformly distributed in the bulk of the window, with boundary layers that shift its mean above the midpoint logarithmically in the window length. The last-passage time through the origin has an exact density, and the time elapsed since it converges to a Gamma law. Reflection symmetry makes the occupation time of the positive half-line and the last-passage time uncorrelated. The results reduce to the Brownian case and agree with simulations.

Once the tilt sufficiently enhances the secondary channel, a robust secondary gap develops and can exceed the primary gap even at weaker effective coupling, while inter-gap coupling locks both gaps to a common critical superconducting temperature. Abstract: Whole-sample and mutual-inductance part-sample measurements of the linear magnetic susceptibility, $χ$, of superconducting films are routinely used to obtain the London penetration depth, $λ(T)$, assuming uniform superconductivity. We show that detectable screening appears only below the percolation temperature, $T_p$, and the transition broadens with the spread of $T_c$. For s-wave domains, $λ(T)$ remains activated for any $T_{c,\min}>0$. Only for $T_{c,\min}=0$ does it become a power law, $Δλ\propto T^n$ with $n\approx1.7$, but then the onset of screening drops to $T_p=T_{c,\max}/2$. Subsequent $α+β$ swaging decreased the compressive yield strength from 1103 to 839 MPa while increasing plastic deformability, with a maximum fracture strain of 32%. Radial hardness heterogeneity was progressively eliminated. These results demonstrate that induction-assisted rotary swaging in selected phase fields provides an effective route for tailoring microstructure, texture, and mechanical response in dual-phase zirconium alloys.

Abstract: Powder X-ray diffraction (XRD) is the primary high-throughput method for resolving crystal structures, a cornerstone of materials science. As high-throughput synthesis and self-driving laboratories advance, automated XRD pattern analysis has become vital for accelerating materials discovery. However, traditional matrix factorization methods fail when lattice variations cause non-linear peak shifts. At the same time, supervised learning approaches face a combinatorial explosion when handling multi-phase mixtures. To overcome these challenges, we adopt a self-supervised Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) to learn representation embeddings from 1D diffraction patterns. By optimizing a masked self-prediction loss directly within the latent space, the framework captures high-level crystallographic semantics. Downstream evaluations show that lightweight attentive probes trained on frozen JEPA representations achieve space group classification and lattice parameter estimation accuracy close to much larger supervised vision transformers. Furthermore, latent probing reveals that the learned representation space naturally organizes physical transformations, isolating smooth trajectories for peak broadening. Interestingly, decoding along latent paths between single-phase crystal representations yields realistic multi-phase mixture patterns, demonstrating an emergent continuous structure learned without explicit multi-phase supervision.

Abstract: The state of a vacancy in a semiconductor is normally described by the Fermi level alone. The result is a stability map in Fermi level and hydrogen chemical potential, which the computational hydrogen electrode relates to pH and potential. We parameterize it with spin-polarized density functional theory in 64-atom cells, including the vibrational free energy of bound H and O, for the silicon vacancy, its H, O, and OH complexes, and both vacancies of 3C-SiC. Hydrogen binds cooperatively to the silicon vacancy, which goes directly from bare to fully passivated $V$H$_4$. In contact with water the bare vacancy is never the lowest-energy state. Near neutral pH it binds oxygen from water as the A center, the paramagnetic hydroxyl complex forms below about pH 5 at zero bias, and full passivation to $V$H$_4$ requires cathodic bias. In PBE the Fermi level barely moves these boundaries, because the acceptor levels lie at or above the conduction-band edge. In SiC the first hydrogen binds to the silicon vacancy by 1.97 eV but is unbound at the carbon vacancy (the $Z_{1/2}$ center), so hydrogen from water alone should not passivate it except under cathodic bias.

EBSD reveals a nearly constant grain size, implying a transition from polycrystalline to single-crystalline particles as particle size decreases. In polycrystalline particles, multiple grains transform gradually over an extended temperature interval because grain boundaries act as barriers to the progression of transformation. In contrast, single-grain particles lack preferred nucleation sites, resulting in larger hysteresis, while the absence of barriers to transformation propagation causes the entire particle to transform in a narrow range. These results demonstrate that the size dependence of the FOMST is governed by the interplay between particle size, grain boundaries, and the free particle surface.

Abstract: Structural defects are central to the functionalities of materials. However, conventional approaches to defect control are often slow and imprecise. This work identifies and characterizes a distinct physical phenomenon of electrostructural bistability that provides a rapid and precise pathway for engineering materials with defect-controlled physical properties. A generalized theory of conductivity bistability is developed and experimentally validated to unify two distinct regimes: previously reported electrothermal bistability in weakly polarizable insulators, such as silicon, and electrostructural bistability in strongly polarizable materials, such as TiO$_2$. In agreement with the theory, experiments on TiO$_2$ demonstrate that bulk-homogeneous defect concentrations in electrostructurally bistable materials can be tuned rapidly and deterministically by controlling applied electric current. Abruptly interrupting the current quenches the system into long-lived, room-temperature metastable states with distinct properties. This establishes a practical, rapid, and scalable pathway for defect engineering with direct relevance to nonvolatile memory devices.

Abstract: Structured scientific spectra encode rich physical information while obeying hard constraints, such as conservation laws and characteristic spectral geometry. Accurate prediction of these spectra is central to materials discovery, yet existing methods often treat them as unconstrained sequences and therefore fail to enforce the underlying physical structure. Taking electronic density of states (eDOS) as the prototypical example, we introduce DeepSciReasoner, a general paradigm for predicting scientific spectra under physical constraints. The framework combines structure-aware spectrum decoding with constraint-preserving physical reasoning, allowing predictions to capture rich spectral structure while respecting the underlying physics. We evaluate DeepSciReasoner on eDOS, phonon density of states (phDOS), X-ray absorption near-edge structure (XANES), and Raman spectra, where it substantially improves prediction accuracy while maintaining physical consistency. These results establish DeepSciReasoner as a reusable blueprint for structured scientific spectrum prediction under hard physical constraints.

Abstract: Template-based crystal structure prediction (CSP) transfers a known crystal structure to a new composition by element substitution, so its accuracy depends on retrieving suitable templates, mapping elements and ranking the resulting candidates correctly. Auditing the original TCSP2.0 implementation on the T180 benchmark, we found that 469 of its 2,347 selected structures (20%) have the wrong composition, caused by element mappings that ignore stoichiometric coefficients and by sequential text substitution that cascades or corrupts element symbols. We propose TCSP3.0, which enforces coefficient-preserving mappings, substitutes species simultaneously on parsed structures and validates every candidate's composition and cell. It further adds periodic-number retrieval, soft oxidation-state evidence, radius-compatibility scoring, structural deduplication, and ORB-v3 machine-learning-potential relaxation and energy ranking of its candidates, as used by deep generative CSP baselines. TCSP3.0 achieves Top-1 and Top-20 match rates of 72.2% and 90.6% on T180, compared with 28.3% and 45.6% for TCSP2.0, and a Top-20 match rate of 74.0% on a new 800-target benchmark (T800). On T180 it also outperforms PNcsp+ and deep generative models CrystalFlow and MaskGXT (Top-1 72.2% versus at most 63.9%). Even without relaxation, TCSP3.0 reaches 67.8% at Top-1 and 90.6% at Top-20 in a median of 28 s per target on two CPU cores, about ten times faster than CrystalFlow. Paired ten-arm ablations on T180 and T800 show that most of the improvement comes from the correctness fixes; structural deduplication significantly improves the Top-5 match rate, embedding-based retrieval is required for coverage, and the remaining scoring features have small or null effects.

Abstract: A common view regarding even-order nonlinear optical (NLO) effects, such as second-harmonic generation and the bulk photovoltaic effect, is that the breaking of inversion symmetry is necessary. However, this view relies on the assumption that light interacts with materials solely through its electric field. Here, we demonstrate that interference between the electric and magnetic components of the same light field can induce even-order NLO effects even in centrosymmetric materials. Using charge and spin photocurrents as prototypical examples, we present a unified microscopic theory showing how such electric-magnetic dipole interference (EMDI) drives NLO responses in inversion-symmetric crystals. Bilinear in both electric and magnetic fields, these responses scale linearly with light intensity. Combining symmetry analysis with first-principles calculations, we decompose the EMDI-induced photocurrents into shift-like and injection-like contributions and classify their dependence on crystal symmetry, time-reversal symmetry, and light polarization. Notably, we reveal that these EMDI-induced responses in centrosymmetric materials are weaker than conventional responses in non-centrosymmetric materials roughly by a factor of fine structure constant $α\approx [10^{-3}\text{--}10^{-2}] $. Nevertheless, they remain well within experimental reach. Our work thus unlocks NLO functionalities in centrosymmetric materials for e.g., materials characterization, photonics and optoelectronics. This is vital given that many functionally importantly materials, such as cubic Si and cubic $\mathrm{SrTiO_3}$, possess inversion symmetry.

</details>

Abstract: Machine learning interatomic potentials enable atomistic simulations with near \textit{ab initio} accuracy over large length and time scales. Foundation models extend this capability across the periodic table but inherit the thermodynamic description of the electronic structure methods used to generate their training data. Small errors in relative phase stability can therefore produce large errors in transformation temperatures. We introduce an additive thermodynamic correction layer that shifts phase free energies towards an assessed reference without retraining or modifying the base potential. A first order thermodynamic construction converts the required phase free energy shifts directly into configuration energy targets, which are fitted using a linear atomic cluster expansion and refined iteratively from measured free energies. We apply the method to melting and solid to solid transformations across elements with different crystal structures using a foundation GRACE model. After the correction, all transformation temperatures lie within the prescribed tolerance of the assessed values, including Ca, Ti, and Zr, where a solid to solid transition and melting are corrected together. Static energy curves, bulk moduli, and liquid structure remain close to those of the base model. Vacancy formation energies, thermal expansion, and liquid density depend on the corrected phase balance and change where the correction is large. The method requires neither retraining, differentiable dynamics, nor access to the parameters of the base potential. It provides a modular route for combining transferable atomistic models with assessed phase thermodynamics.

Abstract: Electro-optic materials, whose refractive index changes linearly with an applied electric field (the Pockels effect), set the speed and energy of optical modulators and microwave-to-optical links for quantum computing. Designing better materials requires optimizing composition alongside temperature, strain, and bias field. However, general repositories lack electro-optic tensor data. We present a pipeline that builds such a database from literature following the rule: "no value is better than a wrong value." The pipeline has five stages: physics-aware acquisition, layout-aware extraction via LLM and deterministic table parsers, a three-tier verification gate, cross-paper consensus using median and median absolute deviation, and a noise-robust surrogate model audited by leave-one-material-out (LOMO) testing. Physics enters directly: notation is mapped, point-group symmetry-forbidden Pockels components are rejected, equivalent labels are merged, and values carry provenance tags. In a run over 49 publications, the gate processed 816 candidates into 414 accepted, 215 held for review, and 187 rejected. Against a hand-certified gold standard of 303 values from 16 papers, the run achieved a precision of 0.51 and recall of 0.48 for exact record matches. Across four database snapshots, held-out materials where the surrogate model beat a predict-the-mean baseline grew from 1 of 4 to 9 of 13 as the database expanded from 10 to 22 materials. While not a controlled comparison, this trend supports the premise that a precision-oriented, physics-checked database can power forward models beyond its training set and clarifies requirements for inverse material design.

Abstract: Machine learning across materials science, chemistry, energy, and medicine increasingly relies on literature-extracted datasets due to a lack of curated databases. Drawing on our work building an electro-optic property database, we present a taxonomy of errors organized by their origin and signatures, alongside a layered framework for error detection. In a production run, our verification gate halted 402 of 816 candidate values with typed reasons. Yet, an audit against curated references showed that 30 of 112 accepted values were defective; standard symmetry rules alone caught 21 of these. On measured BaTiO3 and KNbO3 tensors, constitutive identities caught 8 of 10 single-value errors and isolated the corrupted entries. In a controlled benchmark, the combined framework caught 81% of errors at 85% precision, including errors in single-report materials, bringing model accuracy on held-out data to within 3% of a model trained on clean data. Uncleaned data severely impairs discovery: 89% of the twenty largest values were errors, causing AI searches to prioritize false positives. Once bad numbers are controlled, missing measurement conditions emerge as the main limit, as distinct records become indistinguishable without full context. We outline directions for the field, including shared physics rule libraries and self-reporting databases.

Abstract: Artificial synapses that integrate nonvolatile memory with broadband optoelectronic learning are essential for next-generation neuromorphic computing. However, their realization remains challenging because conventional floating-gate memories exhibit limited optical functionality and poor scalability. Here, we report a van der Waals ReS2/hBN/InSe floating-gate synaptic transistor that combines efficient charge storage with broadband optoelectronic learning in a single device architecture. Optical potentiation and electrical depression further enable bidirectional modulation of synaptic weights. Beyond these synaptic functions, the device demonstrates higher-order learning through associative and discrimination conditioning inspired by the Pavlovian learning paradigm, as well as reconfigurable Boolean logic driven by electro-optical inputs. These results establish a multifunctional van der Waals floating-gate platform that integrates nonvolatile memory, broadband optoelectronic learning, and adaptive information processing within a single transistor, providing a promising strategy for compact, energy-efficient neuromorphic hardware.

Abstract: Einstein relations connect carrier mobility to macroscopic transport coefficients and constitute a fundamental link between microscopic dynamics and transport. In phonon hydrodynamics, this connection is conventionally local: the drift-induced thermal conductivity is proportional to the local drift mobility. Here we show that this relation becomes intrinsically nonlocal under spatial confinement. We derive a nonlocal Einstein relation, $κ_H(y) = \int P(y-y')μ(y'),dy'$, in which hydrodynamic thermal conductivity $κ_H(y)$ at one position depends on collective drift mobility $μ(y')$ across the entire cross-section of the channel. In the small-Knudsen-number limit, our nonlocal theory recovers the viscous heat equation and the local Einstein relation. At finite Knudsen number, nonlocality produces intrinsic boundary slip and distinct size-scaling behavior of the drift mobility $μ$ and hydrodynamic thermal conductivity $κ_H(y)$. First-principles calculations for graphite show that nonlocality broadens the channel-size window over which thermal conductivity exhibits superlinear size dependence. Temperature-dependent simulations further show that the nonlocal theory captures the emergence of a hydrodynamic peak in thermal conductivity normalized by ballistic conductance, whereas the local theory fails to accurately predict its magnitude. Our results establish a microscopic connection between collective phonon mobility and heat conduction beyond local hydrodynamics.

</details>

Abstract: Pulsed sputter processes are essential for a wide range of applications from hard coatings to microelectronics. High power impulse magnetron sputtering (HiPIMS) offers unique possibilities for process design due to its high ionized flux fraction. Because of the high peak-current densities involved, correspondingly high plasma densities generate many ions. This enables tuning of kinetic energies by applying accelerating bias potentials. However, some HiPIMS processes have low deposition rates due to the back-attraction these ions experience with the negatively charged sputter target. Recent work in HiPIMS has targeted this problem, exploring complex pulsing schemes such as bipolar, multi-pulse, or chopped HiPIMS. These pulsing schemes introduce another problem: process complexity. As such, the process space, even with coarse intervals quickly blows up to 1E5-1E20 possible process combinations. This calls for efficient and intelligent sampling, which can be enabled by machine-learning algorithms such as Bayesian optimization. In this work we use multi-objective Bayesian optimization (MOBO) to efficiently sample the process space of pulse-packets, targeting high ionization flux fractions and deposition rates. Over 2,000 distinct processes were sampled during this exploration. Surprisingly, we found that many HiPIMS processes, including single pulse, can achieve deposition rates higher than DC references. In fact, at a working pressure of 1.0 Pa and moderate ionization rates of 5-20% we found that ms-scale pulses were ideal. We hypothesize these processes maximize gas rarefaction, creating a low-collision channel between the target and the substrate. These results showcase previously unreported behavior of pulsed sputter processes and challenge a long-held trade-off between film quality and speed in thin-film manufacturing.

</details>

Abstract: Parametric quantum circuits are usually trained by classical, local optimizers, which use the quantum computer only to evaluate the cost function, or its gradient, one point at a time. Quantum Hamiltonian Descent (QHD) takes a different route: in this quantum algorithm for continuous optimization, the parameters become the position of a quantum particle, with the cost function as its potential, and two time schedules control kinetic and potential energy so that the wavefunction explores the whole landscape and tunnels towards the global minimum. QHD, however, assumes a classically computable cost function, and its digital implementation is poorly understood: standard error bounds grow exponentially with the number of qubits per parameter, and the schedules are borrowed from classical optimization. Here we propose to train parametric circuits with QHD entirely on the quantum computer, through an online step in which the qubits encoding the parameters coherently control the circuit on a second register. We prove that, for cost functions with a smooth periodic extension, including those of parametric circuits, the simulation error admits a bound independent of the grid resolution, and that for circuits the bound grows at most linearly with the number of parameters. We also show that the ideal dynamics depends on the two schedules only through a single curve, which controls the adiabaticity of the search, and that the remaining freedom can be used to minimize the gate count. Simulations on benchmark functions and parametric circuits show that, at equal cost, schedules designed in this way markedly increase the probability of finding the global minimum.

</details>

Abstract: Links of rings offer an intuitive picture of multipartite entanglement. Cutting one ring can free some of the others, just as losing a particle, i.e. tracing it out, can disentangle some of the remaining ones. This analogy classifies multipartite entanglement according to which groups of particles stay entangled when others are lost, a question at the heart of robust quantum networks. However, pure states realizing a given link class were known only in special cases. Here, we close this gap. Recasting link polynomials as hypergraphs, we give an explicit formula for pure qudit states and prove that, for every link class, their entanglement under any pattern of particle loss matches the link exactly. The same construction also controls genuine multipartite entanglement, determining exactly which of the groups that stay entangled are genuinely multipartite entangled, and all of them can be made so. As a consequence, we prove the conjecture that $m$-resistant pure qudit states, which stay entangled after the loss of any $m$ particles but become fully separable after the loss of $m+1$, exist for any number of particles and any $m$.

Abstract: Comb inequalities are essential cutting planes for the travelling salesman problem, yet their separation remains computationally challenging. We propose a quantum heuristic separation algorithm for this family of inequalities. The algorithm uses a procedure that prepares a uniform superposition of all combs with a fixed number of teeth using polynomially many gates and qubits. As part of this procedure, we present a circuit that coherently encodes the Hamming weight of a data register in one-hot form in an auxiliary register. The separation algorithm then searches for violated inequalities using the Grover-Mixer Quantum Alternating Operator Ansatz. Its running time is polynomial under prescribed polynomial budgets for the number of QAOA rounds, parameter optimization steps and samples gathered, although we provide no evidence that such budgets suffice to find a violated comb with constant probability when one exists. We provide an explicit circuit construction for the mixer unitary, the full problem Hamiltonian and a resource analysis.

Abstract: Periodic driving provides a powerful route for controlling localization and engineering effective Hamiltonians in quantum systems. A localized one-dimensional Aubry-André chain can be driven into a fully delocalized phase when the entire system is driven below a critical frequency. Here, we ask whether such delocalization can be achieved by driving only a part of the system. We address this question using a two-leg ladder comprising an Aubry-André chain coupled to a periodically driven clean tight-binding chain. Exact numerical calculations show that, starting from a localized static ladder, subsystem driving below a critical frequency cannot achieve complete delocalization. Instead, a mixed phase emerges, with localized and delocalized states coexisting and the fraction of delocalized states varying nonmonotonically with the driving frequency. This behavior can be understood using local effective Hamiltonians. Although these effective Hamiltonians are derived analytically in the high-frequency regime, they can nevertheless predict the localization transition occurring in the moderate-frequency regime. In fact, by quantifying the emergent nonlocality of the Floquet Hamiltonian, we show that these effective Hamiltonians provide a valid description up to a crossover frequency that is lower than the critical frequency associated with the localization transition. Our results demonstrate an alternative route to Floquet engineering, in which the transport properties of a quantum lattice system can be modified by driving only a subsystem.

Abstract: Quantum simulations on noisy hardware are often designed as if the machine were ideal, with device limitations considered only after the basis and measurement plan have been chosen. We instead use information about the physical problem and the device at an earlier stage, to decide which states and evolution times should form a compact simulation basis. Because the target problem remains unchanged, the method adapts only the representation used to solve it. Tests on many-body and molecular models show that this approach can reveal useful directions that ordinary time evolution misses, while also exposing an important limitation: some of these directions can become too similar to estimate reliably. Realistic device data further show that hardware response can change the preferred states and evolution times before execution. Across seven acquisition days, a four-qubit experiment shows a median 54\% reduction in projected-energy error from problem-informed time choices relative to uniform sampling under matched hardware conditions. These results provide a proof of principle for simulation-basis design as a practical interface between quantum algorithms and the hardware that executes them.

Abstract: Bayes' rule connects forward and reverse processes in classical probability theory, and its quantum analogue has been discussed in terms of the Petz (transpose) map. For quantum dynamics governed by the Lindblad equation, the corresponding Petz map can also be written in Lindblad form. In classical stochastic systems, the analogue of the Lindblad equation is the Fokker-Planck equation, and applying Bayes' rule to it yields the reverse diffusion equation underlying modern diffusion-based generative models. It is known that a semiclassical approximation of the Lindblad equation yields the Fokker-Planck equation for the Wigner function, which is a quasiprobability distribution defined on phase space as the Wigner transform of the density operator. Here we demonstrate that applying the same approximation to the Lindblad equation associated with the Petz map produces an equation that coincides with that obtained from the Fokker-Planck equation via Bayes' rule. This finding establishes a direct correspondence between the Petz map and Bayes' rule, unifying quantum reversibility with classical reverse diffusion.

Abstract: Color-dependent syndrome extraction schedules have been proposed to mitigate hook errors in the triangular color code. Recent work by Kishony and Fowler (arXiv:2603.28852) identifies distance-reducing hook errors by the type of anyon for which they provide a shortcut and chooses the CNOT ordering for stabilizers of each color to avoid these errors. The two data qubits of a junction hook error terminate anyon strings of two different colors at the point where the three colored strings of a logical operator meet, which we call the junction. As such, the hook error does not correspond to a single type of anyon, yet it still reduces the distance. For distances $d=7,9,11$, we verify that every non-corner plaquette admits a scheduled hook error that, together with $d-2$ single data qubit faults, produces an undetectable logical error using $d-1$ faults. Each of these is a junction hook error. Because of this, every stabilizer, including those in the bulk, still requires additional protection from hook errors (such as flag qubits) to restore the full circuit-level distance with this schedule. A minimum weight logical operator has at most one junction, however, so junction hook errors cannot accumulate on one minimum weight logical operator in the way that single-color malign hook errors can. This helps explain why the schedule still improves the circuit-level distance to $d-\lfloor (d+3)/6\rfloor$, rather than the $d/2$ distance associated with spatially uniform color code scheduling.

We relate these extension problems through boundary amplitudes and the semiclassical propagation of coherent states. Abstract: Leggett-Garg (LG) inequalities test whether correlations measured at different times admit a positive joint probability distribution over histories. A violation requires negative values in any joint quasiprobability distribution reproducing those correlations, but such distributions can have negative values even without a violation. Spacetime density kernels extend the density-matrix description to multiple times and are used to study timelike entanglement. We establish a universal bound that relates LG violations to their negative eigenvalue weight. For LG tests involving any number of measurements, we determine the least compatible kernel negativity through an exact relation valid for any system dimension, initial state, dynamics, or measurement rule. This minimum is nonzero if and only if at least one LG inequality is violated. Three-time Lüders LG tests certify at most one eighth of negativity, whereas measurements resolving individual spin levels can approach one half. Including additional incompatible qubit observables gives a further exact minimum that can exceed the LG-only ceiling without increasing the LG violation.

Abstract: Quantum computing offers promising approaches to combinatorial optimization problems whose search spaces grow exponentially with problem size. The Closest String problem belongs to this category, which seeks a string that minimizes the maximum Hamming distance to any string in a given collection of reference strings. In this work, we present an end-to-end quantum implementation of the Closest String problem using the Non-Variational Quantum Walk Optimization Algorithm (NV-QWOA), evaluating its resource requirements and solution amplification capabilities. Our automated workflow converts arbitrary problem instances into quantum circuits expressed in one- or two-qubit gates that encode candidate solutions, evaluate the objective function, and apply NV-QWOA optimisation to increase the measurement probabilities of high-quality solutions. Analytical resource estimates yield a qubit complexity of $\mathcal{O}(nm\log k)$ and a circuit depth of $\mathcal{O}(pm\log^2 n + pn\log n)$, where $n$ is the string length, $m$ is the number of reference strings, $k$ is the alphabet size, and $p$ is the number of NV-QWOA iterations. We introduce a revised prescription for the phase-separation parameter, $γ$, that depends on problem size but requires no instance-specific optimization. This prescription mitigates excessive phase wrapping modulo $2π$, which can weaken the relationship between objective values and the phases guiding the optimization dynamics. Across all simulated problem sizes, the algorithm consistently amplifies optimal and near-optimal candidates. This work provides an explicit gate-level implementation, an improved phase-scaling method, and a foundation for hardware experiments and benchmarking against classical algorithms.

Abstract: Hermitian polynomials in the quadrature operators $x$ and $p$ are the basic ingredients of continuous-variable quantum information, and increasingly exotic ones are now routinely engineered in superconducting circuits and trapped ions. However, such a polynomial need not define unique quantum dynamics: it may define exactly one, none, or an infinite family of physically distinct ones. Such obstructions are invisible to algebraic arguments and can compromise finite-dimensional truncations, which may fail to converge or silently converge to the wrong dynamics. We develop a method, implemented as an algorithm, which decides which case occurs, and how large the family is, from the coefficients alone, with no functional-analytic input from the user. For Gaussian operators the answer is always one; for non-Gaussian ones, a regime continuous-variable quantum computing cannot do without, the other outcomes occur already for simple and physically natural Hamiltonians. We apply the algorithm to operators drawn from the recent literature.

</details>

We compare state infidelity, CNOT gate count, classical runtime, and parallel scalability across different system sizes and bond dimensions. We also introduce a divide-and-conquer environment-aware compilation algorithm (DCE), which optimizes each local isometry approximation according to its contribution to the prepared-state fidelity. The results reveal complementary operating regimes: direct variational compilation methods are gate-efficient for some smaller molecular systems with $O(10)$ qubits and $O(10)$ bond dimensions or moderate accuracy targets with infidelity of order $10^{-1}$, whereas divide-and-conquer methods scale more favorably for larger systems and relatively higher bond dimensions of $O(10\text{-}10^2)$, particularly when target infidelities below $10^{-1}$ are required. Within the latter family, environment-aware optimization improves accuracy over unitary-distance-based compilation while retaining task-level parallelism. For the FeMo-cofactor (FeMoco), we find the DCE algorithm together with the MPS obtained in the entanglement-minimized orbitals (EMOs) provides a resource-efficient route to initial-state preparation. Our results provide practical guidance for selecting and developing MPS compilation methods for strongly correlated molecular simulations.

These results identify relative vibrational displacements as a means of controlling coherent multiphoton conversion and its noise response.

Abstract: Learning with a quantum reservoir can be far simpler than simulating its dynamics. However, state-of-the-art superconducting qubits experience temporal fluctuations in their performance, which limit gate fidelities and quantum processor performance. Abstract: Distributed quantum protocols require measurement outcomes produced at one node to control operations at another. We present QQLink, a programming and orchestration layer that expresses these dependencies in a single program, using workflow nodes, deferred measurement references, and declared entanglement, and derives the cross-node coordination automatically. Its planner constructs a provably acyclic dependency graph in time linear in the total size of the instructions' qubit footprints. The same programs execute through simulator backends or are lowered to dynamic circuits on partitioned quantum hardware. We evaluate seven protocol workflows. Exhaustive feedforward-branch tests on the simulator runtime path, over tomographically complete input sets, yield output-state infidelities of at most $5\times10^{-12}$ and no incorrect classical outputs, and replaying every plan in random topological orders leaves each final state unchanged. Separate executor processes run the plans with delayed messages, with the joint quantum state held by a centralized simulation service. Every run with the full plan is correct, and removing required cross-node dependencies causes protocol failures. The lowered circuits match hand-written implementations of the same construction in two-qubit gate and conditional-block counts. Runs on two IBM Heron processors, each partitioned into virtual QPUs, demonstrate same-chip execution in two sessions a week apart. The runs also show large variation between sessions and losses beyond what calibration-derived noise models predict. Coherence-sensitive checks do not establish coherent Toffoli operation or preservation of the intended repetition-code superposition. These results support QQLink's workflow semantics on the tested execution paths and identify limits of the current hardware realization; execution across physically networked QPUs remains future work.

A synthetic network traffic dataset is generated for the evaluations. The VQC models systematically varied feature encoding methods, parameterized ansatz circuits, quantum neural network types, and classical optimizers. Experimental results show that a trade-off exists between circuit expressivity and optimization stability. Deeper circuits (VQC-4) achieved higher accuracy of upto 89.0\%, while simultaneously being sensitive to training conditions. A shallower variant (VQC-1) under performed, although it had consistent and stable optimization curves. Class-dependent biases were also observed such as in VQC-2, where the model struggled to find an effective decision boundary, and produced a large number of false negatives. The best performing model in this study (VQC-3) combined ZFeatureMap with RealAmplitudes ansatz, optimized using ADAM. Comparative analysis against classical machine learning models such as Support Vector Machine and Random Forest reveal a performance gap, where classical models are able to achieve perfect classification performance on the synthetic data. It is concluded that classical algorithms remain dominant in their ability to classify structured data with relatively simple class separations. However, due to the observed potential of certain VQC configurations to learn decision boundaries to classify data, their utility for future applications in noisier, higher-dimensional problems is highlighted.

Abstract: Photons, trapped ions, neutral atoms, and superconducting circuits are all well-known quantum information platforms. Neutrinos offer a unique possibility, displaying quantum interference (flavour oscillation) over thousands of kilometres, yet interacting so weakly that they can travel through the Earth with minimal interaction. This short paper is a gap analysis. We use information from the existing literature to compare neutrinos to the seven DiVincenzo requirements. Firstly, there are five requirements to do with computation and secondly two requirements to do with communication. We analyse where the gaps are for each requirement. Overall, we find that neutrinos have high fidelity and good coherence; however, they do not have a clear qubit definition and initialisation. They do not have universal gates, individual qubit readout, or stationary-to-flying conversion, meaning they either fail or are very poor. Therefore, we think they are best considered as an exceptional flying information carrier; however, they are very difficult to control. We have outlined all of the gaps which can be used to inform future work.

Abstract: Factored gradient descent (FGD) is a scalable nonconvex method for low-rank positive semidefinite matrix recovery, in which the rank and positivity constraints are satisfied automatically by the factorization. Most of the existing theory that certifies benignity of its loss landscape, however, relies on a restricted isometry property (RIP) of the measurement map. In quantum state tomography, one of its applications, the measurements that can be acquired with stable statistics are limited by detection efficiency and drift to Pauli observables of low weight; we first show that under such partial-weight Pauli measurements RIP fails in the worst possible way. We then bundle the mutually commuting observables setting by setting, pushing the state forward to the probability distribution of each setting, and introduce a max-sliced total-variation loss. On the stratum where the setting attaining the maximum is unique there is no spurious local minimum, and provided only that the measurement map is injective on generic pure states, the global minimum is the truth up to a phase. Finally we verify these claims numerically on four qubits and compare against FGD with the ordinary loss.

</details>

Abstract: Set coherence is a quantum resource for a set of states that characterizes whether a collection of quantum states contains coherence, namely whether these states fail to be simultaneously incoherent in a common basis. In this work, we show that the free operations of set coherence are common commutativity-preserving quantum channels, from which we then establish an axiomatic framework for set-coherence measure (SCM). Within this framework, we investigate the robustness based measures deduced by taking, respectively, the average, maximum, and sum of the single-state robustnesses over a common reference basis, where the first two were previously introduced in the literature and we check here whether they satisfy our SCM axioms. Consequently, we show that the averaged robustness is an SCM but it is neither unified nor complete, the maximal robustness is a unified SCM but is neither complete nor strongly superadditive, whereas the sum robustness is a complete SCM. Furthermore, we propose several classes of SCMs based on pairwise commutators. We show that the Schatten $p$-norm based SCMs are complete and strongly superadditive. We also find that the universal fourth-order Bargmann gap measure proposed in the literature is a complete and strongly superadditive quantifier of set coherence. Finally, we discuss the operational meaning of the SCM in the context of optimal approximate quantum broadcasting, and show that the amount of set coherence can closely reflect the performance of this task.

</details>

Abstract: Free-electron quantum optics has recently emerged as a promising platform for coherent electron-light interactions, yet its potential for quantum-enhanced metrology remains largely unexplored. Here, we investigate the fundamental precision limit of coupling-strength estimation in multi-electron photon-induced near-field electron microscopy (PINEM), demonstrating that the quantum Fisher information scales quadratically with electron number. We further analyze the effect of imperfect electron preparation, showing that electron phase noise is converted into a fluctuating optical displacement and eventually limits the precision. To overcome this limitation, an auxiliary optical mode coupled to the same electron ensemble measures the displacement noise in the signal mode, allowing its conditional suppression and recovery of the Heisenberg scaling. Our work establishes multi-electron coherence as a resource for free-electron quantum metrology and opens the route to precision measurements of nanoscale electron-light interactions.
