Abstract: We construct self-correcting 3D surface codes over $\mathbb Z_q$ with local dimension $q$ growing with system size. The model preserves the local geometry and stabilizer support of the ordinary 3D surface code and remains translation invariant in the bulk, while modifying only the stabilizer Pauli powers. We use the Lee metric to characterize error weight and consider local Metropolis dynamics with single-qudit $X^{\pm1}$ and $Z^{\pm1}$ updates, each changing the energy by at most a constant independent of $q$ and system size $N$. Abstract: In classical simulations of quantum circuits, sampling shots (measurement outcomes) from the final state is a routine but costly step. In widely used software, including Qiskit and PennyLane, its cost grows linearly with the number of shots $S$, even when the simulation has already produced the full state vector, because every shot is generated and processed individually. If only the measurement counts are needed, this is unnecessary. The counts can be drawn directly from a multinomial distribution, decomposed into a sequence of conditional binomial draws, each performed in expected constant time by the BTPE algorithm. The sampling cost is then independent of $S$. We describe an implementation of this approach, which also shortens the computation for states whose probability is concentrated on a few outcomes, and compare it with Qiskit's sampling primitives for up to 20 qubits and $10^6$ shots. Our implementation is faster in every configuration tested, by a factor that grows with the number of shots and exceeds $10^3$ in most configurations at $S = 10^6$, and its samples are statistically indistinguishable from those of the reference implementations.

</details>

On the main benchmark, a general-purpose branch-and-cut solver matches all reference energies and certifies 176 instances within a 600,s cap. When the known optima are moved to randomly chosen bitstrings, the search comparison changes: at n = 48 the branch-and-cut solver finds only four of ten optima and certifies none, whereas parallel tempering reaches every optimum in every tested run at a 60,s budget. These diagnostics do not yield a validated solver-selection rule. While this does not provide evidence for quantum advantage, it identifies a domain for further investigation.

Abstract: We characterize the exact trade-off between internal and external entanglement in asymmetric two-mode Gaussian states at fixed local symplectic eigenvalues. For the non-periodic grid case we use a condition that the true evolution would not result in significant leakage past the boundaries, which introduces basis error unrelated to the resolution of the discretisation. We also show the error in the kinetic energy of a particle scales as $\mathcal O((t+\log N)/N)$ for the plane-wave and grid bases. Together, these results show that $\mathcal O(N^{-1})$ scaling of the error holds for many of the relevant cases for quantum algorithms (with a trivial log factor for kinetic energy).

Abstract: Universal fault-tolerant superconducting quantum computers are expected to require millions of physical qubits; yet extending the prevailing dedicated-control-line paradigm to this scale would exceed the wiring density and cooling-power budget of dilution refrigerators. Microwave frequency-division multiplexing (FDM) revolutionized classical communications by enabling many independent channels to share common transmission infrastructure. Translating this principle to superconducting quantum control, however, requires an all-microwave architecture and experimental validation of frequency-multiplexed gate control. Recent work in quantum error correction has demonstrated microwave-based readout, single-qubit gates and leakage suppression, whereas two-qubit gates remain predominantly driven by unipolar pulses and thus constitute the missing element of an all-microwave architecture. Here, we propose a periodically extensible hybrid multiplexing framework in which all gate operations are frequency-division multiplexed using polychromatic microwave pulses and band-pass filters, thereby reducing the required control-line count by one to two orders of magnitude. In a 1:4 demonstration, we achieve average errors of $(6.4\pm0.2)\times10^{-3}$ for parallel FDM CZ gates and $(10.6\pm0.2)\times10^{-4}$ for parallel FDM $XY(π/2)$ gates, without statistically significant multiplexing-induced degradation. Together with on-chip DC sources, this FDM-based gate-control scheme completes the last missing module in a scalable multiplexed-control architecture toward universal fault-tolerant quantum computing.

Abstract: Dissipative algorithms for preparing thermal and ground states typically require a total Hamiltonian simulation time that grows at least linearly with system size $N$, even when their mixing time grows only polylogarithmically. We achieve sublinear simulation time for free fermions by engineering local interactions with a bath whose size grows in proportion to the system size. Starting from arbitrary initial states, our thermal state preparation algorithm achieves trace-norm error $ε$ in total simulation time $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{N}/ε)$ at fixed inverse temperature and bounded single-particle bandwidth, while ground state preparation of gapped systems requires only $\mathrm{polylog}(N/ε)$ time. We further establish lower bounds for thermal state preparation within the bath protocols considered, identifying two obstacles to faster preparation, namely fourth-order deviations from the target thermal state and fluctuations induced by a random frequency shared by all bath qubits. Our results demonstrate that the linear simulation time barrier in dissipative state preparation can be overcome and clarify the bath and filter-design constraints that govern further complexity improvements.

Abstract: We characterize quasiclassical state conversion under thermal operations through population capacities: the maximum population that thermal operations can concentrate on each set of energy levels. These capacities form a normalized, nondecreasing submodular rank function whose base polytope is exactly the thermally reachable population region. Their bounds therefore provide a complete system of accessibility constraints. Every nested chain of capacities can be simultaneously saturated by a reachable state, yielding the maximum expectation value of any prescribed observable, including the system Hamiltonian. For a prescribed target, its thermomajorization prefixes suffice to certify exact conversion through at most $n-1$ binary work-extraction probes. For approximate conversion, we construct a distinguished reachable state from active capacity constraints using a weighted principal sequence in $O(n^2 \log n)$ operations. At fixed comparison weights, this state minimizes every weighted convex separable residual cost and realizes a common first contact with the corresponding target-centered smoothing regions. Finally, the optimal trace-distance error equals the largest capacity advantage of the target over the initial state. This gap is attained at a target prefix and equals the largest positive optimized energy-storage deficit among the same binary probes, divided by their energy gap. Thus a common population capacity geometry determines accessibility, observable optimization, conversion certificates, and universal approximation.

</details>

Abstract: We study the nonequilibrium steady states of an open anisotropic quantum Rabi trimer. Combining semiclassical and quantum mechanical treatments, we map out the system's phase diagram and elucidate the critical phenomena induced by anisotropic light-matter coupling, cavity dissipation, and complex photon hopping, which give rise to abundant multistable phases. Counterintuitively, the frustrated superradiant phase (FSP) can be stabilized by both positive and negative photon hopping, a feature absent in closed Rabi and Dicke trimers. We further uncover two independent dissipation thresholds for the normal superradiant phase and the FSP. Unlike isotropic open quantum Rabi trimers, both phases are suppressed once the dissipation exceeds their respective critical values. The rotating and counter-rotating interaction terms exhibit mutually reciprocal effects on the quantum phase transitions. By analyzing universal scaling behaviors with quantum fluctuations incorporated, we reveal an unprecedented finite-fluctuation critical exponent for the FSP. Moreover, the critical exponents of individual phases are coupling-strength dependent. The multistability and FSP can be tuned by anisotropy and dissipation. Our results broaden the scope of nonequilibrium quantum phase physics in few-body systems and hold promising applications for quantum state engineering and cavity/circuit quantum electrodynamics.

Abstract: We study the entanglement between an electron and the photon it scatters, resolved in real space and time. Starting from nonrelativistic QED in Fermi's oscillator representation, we write Fock's coupled equations for components with fixed photon number in the photon configuration space, in the length gauge, where the photon is created around the atom through a nonlocal kernel. Truncated at one photon, the equations are solved numerically without the rotating-wave approximation for a one-dimensional model atom in a waveguide with a single transverse mode and enhanced coupling. The atom is driven by a short, off-resonant laser pulse, with the electron and photon represented on real-space grids. The global electron-photon entanglement is largely transient. It peaks during the pulse and decreases as the polarized electron cloud returns to the core, leaving a state close to a product of the electron state and a superposition of the field vacuum with a single scattered photon. Local entanglement, obtained from walkers sampled across both photon-number components, exhibits opposite spatial patterns for the two particles. The electron entropy is smallest at the nucleus and maps the local strength of the dipole coupling, whereas the photon entropy is largest near the atom, where photons are created and absorbed, and smaller in the outgoing wave packets.

Abstract: The earlystage of factory layout planning has a direct effect on the operating costs, efficiency, and productivity of manufacturing systems. Conventional automated planning approaches often face a trade-off between solution quality and computational effort. Recent studies have investigated the potential of quantum annealing (QA) for solving factory layout planning problems. However, existing studies have mainly focused on individual QA-based formulations, whereas current hybrid QA solvers support multiple model types that combine QA and classical methods. To explore the potential of QA for factory layout planning, this paper benchmarks four QA based approaches implemented with the corresponding solvers, including an iterative binary quadratic model (IBQM), a hybrid discrete quadratic model (HDQM), a hybrid constrained quadratic model (HCQM), and a hybrid nonlinear model (HNLM). To improve scalability, an iterative decomposition strategy is developed for IBQM, while a candidate selection procedure is introduced for HDQM. The approaches are benchmarked against genetic algorithm, particle swarm optimization, and a hybrid tabu search and simulated annealing approach on 14 layout instances with different layout sizes, geometric complexity, spatial restrictions, and transport route configurations. The comparative analysis considers solution quality, computation time, and scalability. The results show that QA-based approaches achieve competitive solution quality and substantial computational advantages, reducing computation time by up to 94.51% relative to the classical reference methods. Among them, IBQM obtains the best solution quality in 78.6% of the instances, with a maximum improvement of 17.41% associated with an 81.34% reduction in computation time. These findings provide a basis for their further application in industrial layout planning.

Abstract: The splitting method estimates logical error rates of quantum error-correcting codes deep in the rare-event regime, where direct Monte Carlo sampling is prohibitively expensive. Abstract: Equilibrium propagation (EP) is a powerful training algorithm for self-learning physical systems that is widely used for a variety of machine learning inspired tasks. It relies on the existence of an energy function that depends on tunable training parameters, and can be extended to apply to many-body quantum systems in equilibrium. A key challenge however is the need to repeatedly relax to the system ground state after each parameter update. We show here a route for circumventing this problem by extending EP to many-body driven-dissipative quantum systems, where the dynamics naturally relaxes to a parameter-dependent non-equilibrium steady state. Despite the lack of a free energy, we find that for a class of open systems, a quantum detailed balance symmetry still allows for an efficient EP strategy. This relies crucially on a new class of non-equilibrium static susceptibility relations that we derive in detail. We analyze examples of our new non-equilibrium training paradigm for two experimentally-relevant models: a driven-dissipative transverse field Ising model, and a parametrically-driven bosonic lattice.

For germanium at the experimental temperature, the parameter-free prescription identifying $R_0$ with the spatial quantum spread of a nucleus gives $R_0 \simeq 5 \times 10^{-12}\,\mathrm{m}$. That particular combination, Diósi's stochastic dynamics and the Penrose-motivated mass-density resolution, was therefore excluded. Subsequent searches strengthened the cutoff bound. The Majorana Demonstrator improved it in 2022, and XENONnT has now obtained the world-leading limits $R_0 > 4.9 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$ at $90\%$ confidence level and $R_0 > 4.5 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$ at $95\%$ confidence level. These advances reinforce the 2021 exclusion but do not alter its logical scope: they \emph{parametrically constrain} the Markovian, effectively white-noise DP dynamics, not Penrose's (and Diósi's) lifetime criterion independently of a dynamics, and not all colored-noise, dissipative, or otherwise modified gravity-related spontaneous collapse theories. We reconstruct what Gran Sasso did and did not rule out and place that conclusion in the present experimental context.

In this work, we introduce decoding with alternating weighted graph sparsification (DAWG), an ensemble based approach that combines Tanner graph sparsification with ensembling belief-propagation (BP) decoders. The DAWG decoder runs BP decoders over the full detector error model Tanner graph for certain iterations to then transfer the posteriors to a sparsified graph in which additional rounds of BP are run. Each of the decoders in the ensemble uses a different transfer matrix to map the posterior information to the sparsified graph. We use the serial schedule for the minimum-sum BP decoders and, thus, employ Vibe decoding ideas by randomizing the schedules of each of them. The performance of the DAWG decoder is then numerically studied for bivariate bicycle codes and superdense color code circuits to be compared with other state-of-the-art decoding approaches. Importantly, the DAWG decoder shows a similar performance to Relay belief-propagation and Vibe decoding when combined with an order-$0$ Localized Statistics Decoding (LSD-$0$) post-processor. Crucially, the post-processor is called an order of magnitude less times than for Vibe with the same ensemble size, indicating that the introduced latency tail will be minimal. Furthermore, LSD-$0$ operates on a sparsified decoding graph of a fraction of the size of the full detector error model, which simplifies the resources required by specialized hardware to support it. Overall, the DAWG+LSD-$0$ decoder represents a viable low-cost BP-based decoding algorithm for quantum error correction codes.

Abstract: Discrete nonlinear dynamical systems arise in population models, iterative optimization, and numerical simulations, where computing many iterations can be costly in high dimensions. We study Carleman embedding for forced quadratic discrete systems and its application to quantum state preparation. Under explicit stability and initial-state assumptions, we prove that the truncation error decreases geometrically with the embedding degree, uniformly over all iterations. A central obstacle in discrete time is that the lifted update can become dense even when the original coefficient maps are sparse. We overcome this obstacle by constructing an exact auxiliary linear-system representation that applies the coefficient maps one tensor factor at a time, with row and column sparsities bounded independently of the truncation degree. After rescaling, the associated history system has a condition number that grows linearly with the iteration count, uniformly in the truncation degree. These results yield a quantum algorithm for preparing an approximation to the normalized $n$th iterate with constant success probability. Under coherent input-access assumptions, and for fixed sparsity, stability parameters, and a known positive lower bound on the final-state norm, the oracle query complexity is linear in $n$ and polylogarithmic in the inverse accuracy. Numerical experiments in one and three dimensions illustrate the convergence of the truncated embedding.

Abstract: We study discrete-time quantum walks with Fourier, Grover, and Hadamard coin operators on disordered square lattices, alongside classical random walks. Edges in the lattice are absent with a probability, $p$. In this case, both classical and quantum walks exhibit an essentially identical degradation of transport. We then study a general version of this model, in which the existing edges are retained. However, the absent edges are no longer absent, but now become partially transmitting, with a transmission coefficient $f$. We find that the response is now quite sensitive to the walk protocol and coin symmetry. The classical walk exhibits asymptotic stability of the anomalous diffusion exponent, $β(t)$. In comparison, quantum walks exhibit a monotonically decaying $β(t)$ beyond the initial transient regime, leading to the absence of quantum diffusion in two dimensions. We map the transport regimes in the $(f,p)$ plane and identify the contour for $β=1$, which separates the subdiffusive and superdiffusive regimes. We also examine the effective diffusion coefficient, the inverse participation ratio, and the first two moments of edge transmission. We restrict ourselves to analyzing bulk propagation prior to finite-size boundary reflections. The results indicate that quantum transport depends significantly on coin-specific interference and cannot be fully described by transmission statistics alone.

</details>

Abstract: Quantum networks are built from independent sources, and their nonlocality has mostly been studied with sources that emit bipartite entangled states. A source of three entangled qubits is richer, since its nonlocality can be tested by the Mermin or the more demanding Svetlichny inequality. We ask how network nonlocality relates to these two single-source notions and find that the answer depends on the wiring. When two central nodes without inputs share every source, Bell-state measurements turn every Svetlichny violation into a network violation, and even all entangled generalised Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger states violate the network inequality. When a single central node is shared, the relation reverses, and a network violation requires a Mermin violation of the source. With more sources, the maximal violation depends on whether their number is even or odd, and we give tight bounds for both cases. These results show that the way multipartite sources are wired decides which kind of nonlocality a network inherits.

</details>

Abstract: We investigate the ground state spectrum of a dynamical scalar-fermion-gauge system with an SU(2) gauge group, a scalar doublet, and two degenerate generations of vectorial fermions under the influence of the Brout-Englert-Higgs effect. To this end, we employ lattice methods using the HiRep code. We study the dependence of the system on the (bare) fermion mass and find that the spectrum remains unaffected, up to trivial fermion mass effects. Abstract: We investigate the two-particle correlation function in a finite volume. First, defining an unphysical complex-energy sheet of the correlation function and identifying unstable states as poles on the sheet, we demonstrate that the expansion of the correlation function, which includes poles of unstable states, holds up to exponentially decreasing corrections with the volume size. Based on this result, we show that the finite-volume correction to the complex energy of the unstable state also decreases exponentially with the volume size, namely that finite-volume corrections are relatively small for the unstable states. Then, we show that the imaginary-time correlation function in a finite volume approaches that of the infinite volume as $τ/L^2 \rightarrow 0$ while it is dominated by the lowest energy mode as $τ/L^2 \rightarrow \infty$, where $τ$ is the imaginary time, $L$ is the volume size. In drawing these conclusions, we first present a qualitative, general discussion and then demonstrate quantitative results using simple nonrelativistic models with a contact interaction (EFT) and a separable potential.

Abstract: When the Boltzmann weight is nonnegative, the actions of a lattice field theory define a family of probability distributions that forms a statistical manifold, and its Fisher metric gives the number of measurements needed to distinguish two nearby actions. In the large-$N$ limit of the two-dimensional lattice Gross-Neveu model with Wilson fermions, we study the continuum limit, in which the lattice spacing $a$ is taken to zero with the mass gap $Λ$ fixed, through the Fisher metric of the distribution of the auxiliary field $σ$. The renormalization condition fixes an expectation coordinate, and dimensional transmutation becomes an exponential relation between it and a natural coordinate. The main result is that the $a\to0$ limits differ between the Fisher metric of the long-distance modes, whose momenta are fixed in physical units, and the Fisher metric of the full $P(σ)$. In the former, the lengths of the infinitely many irrelevant directions that change the kinetic term shrink, relative to the mass-term direction, to order $aΛ\log(1/(aΛ))$ or smaller, and when several modes are combined, only the two directions $(Λ,m)$ of the continuum theory remain. In the latter, the kinetic-term directions shrink at the same rate as the mass-term direction, and the difference in the regularization remains in correlations at distances of the order of the lattice spacing. An exception is the deformation that only adds a contact term to the propagator: as $a\to0$ its length goes to zero in both metrics, and it becomes indistinguishable from a change of the coupling $1/g^2$ (a lattice version of Kutasov's result). The curvature of the coupling plane at fixed kinetic term diverges as $(1/2)\log^2(1/(aΛ))$, not as the correlation volume often assumed in thermodynamic geometry, and the critical point is infinitely far in Fisher distance from any point at finite $a$.

</details>

Abstract: We explore a method to extract pion vector form factor from three-point functions using currents constructed from LapH-smeared quark fields. LapH employs the low eigenmodes of the Laplacian Heaviside operator on the lattice to smear out the quark fields in spatial directions. The LapH-smeared quark propagator retains all symmetry properties of the original one and can be re-used to construct correlation functions at a reduced cost, with equal treatment of connected and disconnected diagrams. The calculations are performed on a two-flavor dynamical nHYP-smeared ensemble on the $24^3\times 48$ lattice at a spacing of 0.12 fm and pion mass of 315 MeV. We show that an extrapolation in the number of low eigenmodes in the LapH subspace can reproduce the same form factor computed from the conventional SST method with point source, sink, and current. The study opens up the possibility of applying LapH-smeared currents to extract hadron form factors and polarizabilities including disconnected diagrams.

Abstract: We present the first computation of the $π^+π^+$ scattering length in large-$N$ QCD in the 't Hooft limit, $N_{\scriptscriptstyle{\rm f}}/N\to 0$, using the twisted Eguchi--Kawai formulation of the lattice theory. We employ values of $N$ up to 1156 and consider several quark masses and lattice spacings to achieve controlled large-$N$, continuum, and chiral limits. The chiral behavior of the scattering length is compared with next-to-leading-order Chiral Perturbation Theory predictions. Our result for the low-energy coupling describing the sub-leading quark-mass dependence is consistent with existing S-matrix bootstrap bounds. Combining TEK results with previous lattice QCD data obtained using standard methods for $3\le N\le 6$, we are also able to extract sub-leading $\mathcal{O}(1/N)$ corrections to the large-$N$ scattering length.

Abstract: The recently discovered IR phase of QCD is characterized by an infrared (IR) sector of Dirac modes that separates and dynamically decouples from the bulk of the spectrum, becoming scale invariant in the process [1]. Among key features of this IR sector is the negative power-law behavior of Dirac spectral density near zero with power $p$ close to $-1$. Here we start investigating to what extent can this behavior be mimicked by chiral Anderson models. In particular, we study 3-D chiral-orthogonal systems with logarithmic off-diagonal disorder where negative-power ``spikes'' have been observed [2]. We find that at finite disorder strength $W$ of moderate strength, these models in fact do not have a power-singular density of states (DOS) at zero energy in the thermodynamic limit. Rather, this power $p_1$ vanishes and the spectrum features a very accurate power behavior with $p<0$ on an interval around $E = 1$ spreading increasingly closer to $E=0$ and $E = \infty$ with increasing $W$. We connect this to the fact that random hoppings $t$ in these quantum systems are drawn from a scale-invariant $1/t$ distribution regularized via $t \in [e^{-W/2}, e^{W/2}]$, and DOS with negative power can naturally arise inside that range. We show that $\lim_{W \to \infty} p(W, E)=-1$ for all energies accessible by our data. This indicates that, upon removing the cutoff $W$, quantum mechanics transforms the scale-invariant $1/t$ noise into a scale-invariant $1/E$ distribution of eigenstates, which mimics the interpretation of scale-invariant Dirac spectral densities in the IR phase as being due to scale-invariant glue backgrounds [1]. Our results imply that, to model the IR sector of the IR phase, the IR cutoff in chiral Anderson systems needs to be viewed as descending from the randomness strength $W$ rather than the lattice size $L$.

</details>

Abstract: Machine learning methods have recently gained increasing interest as possible means of solving critical slowing down in lattice field theory, with particular emphasis on sampling methods using field transformations such as normalizing flows, annealing/out-of-equilibrium methods, and diffusion models. Annealing methods provide appealing polynomial scaling and exactness guarantees, while diffusion models use a simple and efficient score-matching training procedure. This work explores a method combining these benefits, first training an energy-based diffusion model using score matching, then sampling using either stochastic normalizing flows (SNFs) or flow-transformed replica exchange (T-REX). The choice of sampling method can be made after training the diffusion model; the theoretical properties of SNFs and T-REX and their relative trade-offs are discussed. Numerical results are presented for scalar field theory and the O(3) non-linear sigma model, using a class of local-footprint energy models with efficient gradient and divergence calculations. Sampling from the trained models follows predicted exactness and scaling properties, and significantly improves over sampling methods without a learned component.

</details>
